Flaw 23's remedy, each part taken out on its own, in a copy of the module not kept, once it was what every instance does: the folder's wait, `ViewNamesSettled` in `FlawViewFolderRenameBack` (176,783 states), its liveness instance (176,681) and `ViewFolders` (271,646); the copy's wait, nothing in any instance (the flaw's pool 62,523), as the walks here decide a disk at a time; the pass's rename, `ViewNamesSettled` in the flaw's pool and its liveness instance (54,718 each), `ViewFolders` (230,526) and `ViewFoldersUser` (336,682), and liveness in `FlawViewFolderRenameBackLive` (148,070) and `ViewFoldersUserLive` (8,171); the take-in's wait for the files below the old name, `ViewNamesSettled` in the flaw's pool (64,101), its liveness instance (63,825) and `ViewFolders` (275,211). In the code, each part's tests fail without it, but the folder's wait's: without the copy's wait, `TestARenameBackMissedByALostDiskIsKept` in each of its 14 cases, as the pass decides the two copies found moved to two names together; without the rename, those and four of observation 5's (`TestARenameBackThroughTheViewWhileAPassDecidesTheFirst`, `TestARenameBackThroughTheViewNoPassRecorded`, `TestARenameBackThroughTheViewAfterAPassFoundTheFirst`, `TestARenameBackThroughTheViewWithT2Away`), whose copies the waits then hold for good; without the take-in's wait, `TestARenameBackThroughTheViewWaitsForACopyGone`; without the folder's wait, none. A folder found renamed waits in the code for its own disk's copies below it, which the copy's wait holds, so the folder's wait matters there only for a real rename whose copies below the new name were decided before, which no test makes; the model, deciding a disk's folder beside its copies, needs it, and TLC's trace, `TestARenameBackThroughTheViewWhileAPassDecidesTheFirstWithT1Lost`, passes in the code either way.

### What it leaves out

The daemon's own journal and its crashes (each of its operations one step here; `Folders.tla` and `Replication.tla` have their steps); the guards and the settle; two copies of a file found moved to two names in one pass, which the code decides together, the second a file of its own (`ActOtherTarget`), where the model decides them a disk at a time (flaw 23, why its (b) holds in the model and not in the code); what a user makes new being recorded as a file of its own; the power cut (every name durable as it is made); refused and colliding names; folders below folders; the view's other operations (`Replication.tla`'s and `ViewMove.tla`'s); more than two disks; a folder's mode.

## Folders below folders, and names a disk refuses: Nested.tla

A model of its own, `Nested.tla` with `MCNested.tla` (2026-11-24), for what `Folders.tla` leaves out: folders below folders, and names a disk's filesystem refuses (§15.8). `Folders.tla`'s paths are a folder at the root and a file in it, which every one of its steps reads; nested paths there would mean the module written again and its instances, already among the largest of the set, multiplied, so the two items are a module of their own, which takes `Folders.tla`'s passes as they are and each operation of Atoll as one step (journaled, made and committed at once), their steps and reprise being `Folders.tla`'s and `Rename.tla`'s. A path is a folder's, one or two names deep (D, E, D/E, E/D...), each folder holding one file; on each of two disks the folders and their files, by object, and the trash; the catalog's files and copies (where each sits, its `old_lpath`), the entries of the folders, the folder jobs of each disk and the trash jobs `reason = renamed`. A user, on a disk, moves a folder anywhere, into another, out of one or renamed, with its subtree; makes a folder; or moves a file to another folder; a disk refuses some names, as NTFS a name with ":", which neither a user nor Atoll can make there. Atoll killed between any two steps, and a disk lost and mounted again. A pass: the walk of each disk, its verdicts and the folders found renamed (`watch.moveFolders`: a folder row whose folder is not at its name, found at a name with no row, not below the old one, the old name holding no file at home), a folder and its subfolder each found so; the decisions about the files, `Folders.tla`'s; the folders found renamed in the order of their jobs, each waiting while its disk still holds copies below the old name, its entries rewritten below the new one (`catalog.rewriteEntries`), the jobs whose target is at or below the old name moved on (`catalog.moveFolderJobs`), one whose two names become equal going, and a job for each other disk with a copy or an entry below it; then on each disk its trash jobs, its folder jobs in the order of `jobs.folderOrder` (`waitsFor`, the first of a cycle dropped), each run as one operation when ready (`jobs.folderReady`: the folder its entry records at the old name, the new absent and not below the old, its name not refused, `landsBadly`, its missing parents made) and dropped otherwise, the copies then following one by one; the renames of copies behind, round after round, a copy below a job queued waiting for it, its missing folders made, a name its disk refuses leaving it behind (§15.12, Refused names); and the trash jobs those want once another copy of their file is at its path on a disk walked in that pass (`findWishes`), which the next pass runs if that still stands (`stillStands`).

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `NoUserDataLost` | every version a user wrote is at a path, or in a trash, of some disk |
| `LastCopyKept` | 2: a copy goes to the trash for a name its disk refuses only while another copy of its file is at the file's path |
| `RenamesAreUsers` | §15.12: a file takes only a path a user moved one of its copies to, directly or with its folder, or where a folder's job carrying out a user's folder rename put it (everything below moves with it, §15.12) |
| `OneRowAnObject` | 13 on one disk: no object is the copy of two files |
| `EventuallyFollowed` | liveness, with fairness: every copy at its file's path on its disk, but one whose name its disk refuses, behind or in the trash; no job left; each file where its catalog says on some disk |

Measured on 2026-11-24 with `test/tla/run.sh`, 2 workers, other agents' containers on the machine, in the run of the whole set (above):

| Instance | Expects | What it explores | States | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `NestedInto` | pass | D and E on both disks, D holding a file: a user acting twice, on either disk, a folder moved into another, out of one or renamed, made, or the file moved; Atoll killed once, a disk lost once | 272,990 | 12 s |
| `NestedBelow` | pass | D on both disks: a user acting three times, as D renamed to E, a new D made and E moved into it, which moves the job D to E on to D/E, below its own old name | 1,083,470 | 38 s |
| `NestedSub` | pass | D and D/E on both disks, a file in each: a folder moved with its subtree, or its subfolder alone, twice | 166,464 | 9 s |
| `NestedRefused` | pass | D on both disks, disk 2 refusing the name E: a user acting three times; the folder's job refused there, the copy left behind, its trash job once the other copy is at the file's path | 345,806 | 13 s |
| `NestedIntoLive` | pass | liveness: `NestedInto`, the user on disk 1 alone | 70,499 | 10 s |
| `NestedBelowLive` | pass | liveness: `NestedBelow`, the user on disk 1 alone | 204,884 | 25 s |
| `NestedSubLive` | pass | liveness: `NestedSub`, the user on disk 1 alone | 51,336 | 11 s |
| `NestedRefusedLive` | pass | liveness: `NestedRefused`, the user on disk 1 alone | 232,739 | 26 s |

TLC found no flaw. Each pool reaches what it is there for, checked on trial pools of the same shape by an invariant that must break, in a copy not kept: a job whose target is below its own old name (`NestedBelow`'s), one whose target a further rename left past two names (`NestedInto`'s), a trash job of a refused copy, and the copy in the trash (`NestedRefused`'s).

### Modelling mistakes, found by TLC and fixed

- A further rename at or below a job's target can leave it deeper than two names (D to E/D, then E moved to D/D, the job D to D/D/D), which the code holds as any path. The model's filesystem goes two names deep: such a job is refused there, as a branch's limit would refuse it (`fitsBelow`), and goes, its copies following one by one; and a copy behind no longer where its row says, below a folder its disk no longer holds, keeps its place where the code would rewrite it past two names, the next walk finding the copy where it is either way.
- `RenamesAreUsers` read as Atoll's the path a folder's job gave a file below a subfolder a user had renamed on that disk (a user renaming D to E on one disk, and D/E to D/D on the other, the job then carrying D/D to E/D): everything below moves with the folder (§15.12), and the walk after reads that subfolder's name as the user's, which it is. The job's landing places count as a user's now, as `Folders.tla` takes the latest name a user gave a folder.
- The liveness pools let a user act on both disks, which can leave two files claiming one path for good (a user moves D/E out to E on one disk while another renames D to E on the other): every version is kept, and one file waits `path-taken`, as §15.12 says, until the user settles it. The liveness instances let the user act on disk 1 alone (`UserDisks`); the safety ones on both.

### How sensitive it is

Each taken out on its own, in a copy of the module not kept: the trash job's check that another copy is at the file's path (`LastCopyKept` in `NestedRefused`'s trial pool, 256,654 states, and its liveness pool, 164,796); the copies of a disk behind below a folder following its job (`EventuallyFollowed` in `NestedIntoLive`, `NestedBelowLive` and `NestedSubLive`). Nothing broke without these, in any instance: the jobs moved on by a further rename, the wait for a disk's copies below the old name, the order of a disk's jobs, the job taking only the folder its entry records, `landsBadly`, and the name check of a copy's rename or of a folder's job, as the filesystem refuses what the check would, and the passes then take the same course one step later. These are the mechanisms whose absence costs a pass or a needless operation here, not a name or a version: what would show it, an invariant of the operations made, the model does not state.

### What it leaves out

The steps of each operation and their reprise (`Folders.tla`'s, `Rename.tla`'s), the power cut, content beyond a version, edits and conflicts (`Replication.tla`'s), deletions and the guards, the debounce (every change is due at the walk that finds it), swaps and cycles of files and their exchange (`Folders.tla`'s), the plan and the copies it makes (the file a refused copy leaves short is `name-incompatible` through the plan, §15.8), names that collide (§15.7), the branch's limit but for two names of depth, more than two disks, a folder's mode, and Atoll's own folders left empty.

## Name collisions on NTFS: Collisions.tla

A model of its own, `Collisions.tla` with `MCCollisions.tla` (2026-11-25), of §15.7's hard constraint of the solver on an NTFS disk (§12.4, §15.8): Atoll never writes there a name that collides, at some component, with another of the same `lpath_key` and other bytes. Two disks: S, the source, whose filesystem holds every name, and N, an NTFS disk, the target of each file's replica. Folder names Films and films share a key, Other has its own; file names a and A share one where an instance says so. N's driver finds a name by its bytes, as every driver Atoll writes to does, ntfs3, the type `ntfs`, ntfs-3g and lowntfs-3g as the mount check takes them (a short name aside, §15.8), or by its key (`Fold`), as ntfs3 and the type `ntfs` with `nocase`, and lowntfs-3g with `ignore_case`, would, which the mount check refuses (§10). The plan places each replica when no name collides by what the catalog has (`catalog.KeysTaken`: the files with a copy on N at their name, pending ones included, the names copies behind still sit at, and N's entries, for the folder then for the file) and by what the plan holds itself (`solver.ownNames`: the folders above a pending copy, the name a copy behind is to take, with its folder, and what this plan placed), its path free. What it queues runs at any time after it, as the copies of a pass, of `atoll replicate --now` or of copies at once do: a copy's job (`jobs.copier`: its look at the target, `ops.LookAtTarget`, its missing folder made, the part moved to the file's path as it is then); the rename of a copy behind (`jobs.runRenames`, refused by the catalog's keys but for its old name, `catalog.KeyTakenExcept`); a folder's job (`jobs.runFolderJob`, the same for its new name); and a new file through Atoll's own view, which lands on N when N takes its name by the catalog (`writer.refusedOn`), on S otherwise, and which the catalog takes in at the next walk. Right before each name Atoll makes on N, it lists the folder the name goes in, and each folder on the way, and refuses the name when one holds another of its key, whoever made it (`DiskCollides`, the maintainer's remedy of flaws 24 and 25, below): a copy's job goes, a rename or a folder's job keeps its place, the view's new file goes to S. A user renames a file or a folder on S, which the passes take to N, writes a new file through the view, or makes a folder on N directly.

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `NoAtollPair` | §15.7: N never holds two names with one key, both written by Atoll |
| `WritesNoCollision` | §15.7, §12.4: Atoll never writes a name that collides with one N holds, whoever made it, one no walk found yet included (until flaws 24 and 25 were fixed, only Atoll's or one the catalog recorded, §15.7's stated limit then) |
| `CopiesAtTheirNames` | 6: a copy Atoll wrote on N is at its name, to the byte, where the catalog says it sits |

§15.7 also says that nothing is changed automatically: no action of Atoll, in the model as in the code, renames or removes a name to end a collision. A folder a user made directly on N is renamed only by a folder's job, which takes there a rename the user made of that very name on S (§15.12), as TLC showed once a check of it flagged that (Modelling mistakes, below).

Measured on 2026-11-25 with `test/tla/run.sh`, 2 workers, in the run of the whole set once flaws 24 to 26 were fixed (above):

| Instance | Expects | What it explores | States | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `Collisions` | pass | Films/a on S, Other/a on S and N, a third file the view may make, file names a and b: a user renaming a file or a folder on S, writing a new file through the view or making a folder on N, four times; N finds names by their bytes | 282,868 | 7 s |
| `CollisionsFold` | pass | `Collisions`' pool, a and A sharing a key, N finding names by their key | 202,911 | 5 s |
| `FlawCollisionRename` | pass | flaw 24, fixed: a rename on S taken to N before the copy of a file of the folder of the other case | 69 | 1 s |
| `FlawCollisionView` | pass | flaw 24, fixed: a new file through the view, not taken in, before the plan | 11 | 1 s |
| `FlawCollisionMoved` | pass | flaw 24, fixed: a file renamed on S while its copy waits, a and A sharing a key | 63 | 1 s |
| `FlawCollisionFold` | pass | flaw 25, fixed: a user's folder of another case made on N, which N's driver finds by its key | 13 | 1 s |

Each flaw had a test of the code behind the build tag `flaws`, in `internal/jobs/flaws_test.go`, seen failing (`make model-flaws`), as each needs the window between the plan and a copy's job, which no crash point of the conformance build opens; they are regular tests now (below). The remedies proposed were entries of `Fixes`, each checked by TLC on 2026-11-25 against every invariant, in every instance of the module; the one the maintainer chose is what every instance does, and `Fixes` is empty.

### Flaw 24: a name brought onto an NTFS disk between the plan's check and the copy's write collides

Found on 2026-11-25 by `NoAtollPair`. Fixed on 2026-11-25 with the maintainer's remedy, (a) `disk-key-look` (The remedy, below).

§12.4: "on an NTFS target, no name collision with the names on that disk"; §15.7: "Atoll never writes a colliding name to an NTFS disk".

The plan checks a copy's names once, when it places it, by the catalog's keys and by what it holds itself; the copy waits in the queue. Nothing checks them again: not a later plan, which keeps a pending copy without asking its names, nor the copy's job, which writes the file's path as it is when it runs. And every other writer asks the catalog alone, which has no row of the folder above a copy not made yet, nor of a name the view wrote that no walk took in yet. So Atoll writes two names with one key on N, three ways, each an instance:

- `FlawCollisionRename` (70 states): Films/a on S, Other/a on S and N. The plan places Films/a on N, its folder Films held by the plan alone. A user renames Other/a to films/b on S; a pass takes it to N, as the catalog has no row of Films: films is made there. Then the copy makes Films beside it.
- `FlawCollisionView` (10 states): a new file films/a through the view lands on N; before any walk takes it in, a plan places Films/a on N, the catalog having no row of films; the copy makes Films beside it.
- `FlawCollisionMoved` (64 states), a and A sharing a key: the plan places Films/a on N; a user renames it on S to Other/A while its copy waits; the walk takes the rename, the pending copy following the file; the copy writes Other/A beside Other/a.

`TestFlawACopyNotMadeYetCollidesOnNTFS` (build tag `flaws`, then; now `TestNoNameBroughtOnNTFSBeforeACopyCollides`, with the view's way too) ran the first and a folder form of the third in the code, the copy queued and run later by `atoll replicate --now`: t2, an NTFS disk, ended with Films and films, both written by Atoll.

Remedies proposed, each checked by TLC on 2026-11-25:

- (a) `disk-key-look`: right before Atoll makes a name on an NTFS disk, a folder it makes, a file it places, a rename's or a folder job's new name, a new file or folder of the view, it lists the folder it writes in and refuses the name when that folder holds another with the same key, whoever made it, as it looks for the short names of §15.8: the disk is the truth, what no row says yet included. It holds alone, in every instance, flaw 25's too (`Collisions` 282,868 states, `CollisionsFold` 202,911). Its one part is the remedy. In the code, a check of the names of the folder a name is made in, against `names.Key`, in `ops`, beside `Disk.isAlias`, where a copy, a rename, a folder's job and the view make a name on an NTFS disk (`ops.LookAtTarget`, the folders `jobs.makeParents` makes, the folder's rename, `CreateUserFile`, `MakeUserFolder`, the view's rename); a read of that folder for each name made on NTFS, which the short-name check makes only for names of its form; no contract change. For a user: a copy, a rename or a write through the view that would make two names with one key on NTFS is refused when it comes, `name-collision`, the file kept short of that replica or the copy behind, as for a name the plan refuses, whatever the catalog knows yet: §15.7's stated limit no longer binds what Atoll writes.
- (b) by the catalog: `view-names`, the catalog's keys count the names of the view's operations no pass took in yet, on their disk, with their folders (`catalog.KeyTaken` reading `view_ops`); `job-check`, a copy's job asks the catalog's keys of each component of its path right before it writes, but for that path, and goes when one is taken, its file reported `name-collision`; with `exact-look` for flaw 25. Together they hold in every instance (`Collisions` 279,318 states, `CollisionsFold` 173,118). Each part taken out on its own: `view-names`, `NoAtollPair` in `FlawCollisionView` and `Collisions`; `job-check`, `NoAtollPair` in `FlawCollisionMoved`, `WritesNoCollision` in `Collisions`; `exact-look`, flaw 25's. A fourth part, `pending-folders`, the catalog's keys counting the folder above each pending copy, makes the rename's writer refuse films in `FlawCollisionRename`, where `job-check` makes the copy refuse Films after it: nothing breaks without it, and with it the four hold too (`Collisions` 270,534, `CollisionsFold` 162,461). In the code, a query of `view_ops` in `KeyTaken`, and the check of the plan in the copy's job: a few dozen lines, no contract change. For a user: the same, but for a name made directly on N since its last walk, which stays §15.7's stated limit.

### Flaw 25: on a driver that finds a name by its case alone, a copy lands in a user's folder of another case

Found on 2026-11-25 by `CopiesAtTheirNames` in `CollisionsFold`'s pool; `FlawCollisionFold` (15 states). Fixed on 2026-11-25 with flaw 24's remedy, (a) `disk-key-look` (The remedy, below). It was taken for §15.7's stated limit seen on ntfs3: it is not quite. ntfs3, as Atoll writes to it, finds a long name by its bytes, as ntfs-3g and the type `ntfs` do (`docs/research/ntfs-driver.md`, Case and short names), and only the variants the mount check refuses fold (§10). So no driver Atoll writes to has the copy land in films; on each, the trace had the copy make Films beside the user's films, the stated limit itself, which the remedy closes too (`TestNoCollidingNameOnEachNTFSDriver`, below).

§15.7: "A colliding name made on an NTFS disk since its last walk, which has no row yet, is not seen; the next walk reports it"; invariant 6: a replica has exactly the same relative path and name as its source.

N's driver finds a name by its case alone, as a variant the mount check refuses does. The plan places Films/a on N; a user makes films there directly, which no walk found yet. The copy's look at its target (`ops.LookAtTarget`) asks the driver for Films, which answers films: the copy takes it for its folder, and lands at films/a, while the catalog records it at Films/a. The next walk finds the copy at films/a and none at Films/a, which it reads as a rename made on N. `TestFlawACopyLandsInAUsersFolderOfAnotherCase` (build tag `flaws`, then; now `TestACopyNeverMakesANameBesideAnotherOfItsKey`, on a driver of each kind) ran it in the code on a double of such a driver (`foldingDir`, ASCII case): t2's copy, `ok` at `Films/a.mkv` in the catalog, was at `films/a.mkv`.

Remedies proposed: (a) `disk-key-look`, flaw 24's, which refuses a name whose folder holds another with its key; or `exact-look`, a name a look finds on NTFS taken for that name only when its folder lists it with its bytes, as the short names of §15.8 are (`Disk.isAlias` in `ops`), refused otherwise, `name-collision`. Each holds in every instance with flaw 24's remedy (above).

### The remedy of flaws 24 and 25

The maintainer chose (a) `disk-key-look` on 2026-11-25 (DONE.md): right before Atoll makes a name on an NTFS disk, it lists the folder where the name goes and refuses another name with the same key, whoever wrote it.

**Each folder on the way, not that one alone.** A sensitivity check, made in a copy of the module and not kept, listed the folder the name goes in alone, the folders on the way taken as a lookup finds them: `WritesNoCollision` broke in `Collisions` (13,028 states), and `CopiesAtTheirNames` in `CollisionsFold` (273) and `FlawCollisionFold` (15). The first trace: the view writes films/x on N, films Atoll's and unrecorded; a user makes Films there directly; the plan places Films/a on N, the catalog knowing neither; the copy finds Films there, lists Films alone, and writes a in it, a path that collides at its folder with Atoll's films. The second is flaw 25 itself: a lookup on a driver that folds takes films for Films, and the listing of films finds nothing against a. So the look before each write lists each folder on the way too, what the model's `DiskCollides` does at the root, and decides a name there by that listing, never by what a lookup finds, as for the short names of §15.8; the folder a name is made in is listed right before it is made.

In the code: `ops.collidesIn`, `refuseColliding` and `lookThrough` (`internal/ops/collide.go`), on an NTFS disk only, with `names.Colliding`, the rule of `ntfs_collision.json` for one folder, which compares two ASCII names without their keys. `lookThrough` replaces `isAlias` in `LookAt` (now with the path a rename leaves, so that a case-only rename does not collide with its own old name) and `LookAtTarget` (`TargetCollides`), and in the view's `userParent`; `refuseColliding` runs right before `MakeRootFolder`'s mkdir, `PlacePart`'s, `FromTrash`'s, `RenameFile`'s and `RenameFolder`'s moves, and the view's `CreateUserFile`, `MakeUserFolder`, `MakeUserSymlink`, `RenameUser`, `PlaceUserPart` and `LinkUser`: `ErrNameCollides`, an `ErrNameRefused`. A copy so refused ends `name-refused` and its file waits, as for a name its driver refuses, until the walk that records the other name lets the plan report `name-collision`; a rename or a folder's job keeps its place; a restore ends `name-collision`; what the view makes goes to the next disk placement gives without that one (`writer.elsewhere`), and its rename is refused, `EINVAL`, before any disk renames (`writer.collidesOn`). A listing that fails is the disk unknown (invariant 4). No contract change: `name-refused` and `name-collision` are outcomes the `--json` schemas have. specs.md says it in §15.7, §15.8 and §17.4. What it costs: a listing of each folder on the way at each look on NTFS, and of the folder a name goes in as it is made, which grows with the folder; in qemu with no KVM, a look at a name in a folder of 2,000 names, as `TestScale`'s at 200,000 files a disk, took 4 to 12 ms with the folder cached and 10 to 42 ms without, on ntfs3, the type `ntfs`, ntfs-3g and lowntfs-3g on Linux 7.3, beside 0.7 to 0.95 s for the copy of 1 MiB there, its looks included (`TestWhatAKeyLookCostsOnEachNTFSDriver`).

The tests: `TestNoNameBroughtOnNTFSBeforeACopyCollides` (flaw 24's three ways, the view's among them), `TestACopyNeverMakesANameBesideAnotherOfItsKey` (flaw 25 on a driver of each kind, and a folder on the way), `TestTheViewMakesNothingBesideANameOfItsKey`, `TestAKeyLookThatFailsWritesNothing` and `TestARenamesKeyLookThatFails` (`EIO` on each new listing), `TestAStopBeforeAKeyLook` and `TestAStopBeforeARenamesKeyLook` (kill -9 right before each), `internal/jobs/collisions_test.go`; `TestEachNameMadeOnNTFSIsLookedAtByItsKey` and `TestTheLooksOnNTFSSeeTheKeys`, `internal/ops/collide_test.go`; the scenarios above; `TestNoCollidingNameOnEachNTFSDriver` on a real volume of each NTFS driver, on Linux 6.12 and 7.3. In the model, `DiskCollides` is what every instance does, `Fixes` is empty, `WritesNoCollision` asks it of every name N holds, whoever made it, and the flaws' instances are regression instances, expecting pass. For a user: Atoll never writes a name to an NTFS disk that collides with another there, whoever made that one and whatever the catalog knows yet; §15.7's stated limit binds only what the plan reports before the next walk.

### Modelling mistakes, found by TLC and fixed

- A case-only rename read as colliding with its own old name: §15.7 says it does not, and the name the rename frees is left out of what it is checked against now.
- A check that a folder a user made directly on N is never renamed by Atoll flagged a folder's job that took there a rename the user made of that very name on S, which §15.12 makes a rename to follow; the check went, as no action of Atoll renames or removes a name for a collision.
- A file the view made on N, which no walk took in yet, had no path in the catalog, and its copy read as one behind its path.
- The driver that finds a name by its key was said to be ntfs3, as Atoll writes to it; it is none Atoll writes to (flaw 25, above). `Fold` stays, a driver the mount check refuses, as what a remedy deciding by the listing must hold against.

### What it leaves out

Everything else of a pass and of a copy (`Replication.tla`'s, `Copies.tla`'s), the steps of each operation and their reprise, crashes, disks lost, deletions, names a disk refuses (`Nested.tla`'s), folders below folders, a user's own files on N, the trash and its restores, the SSD cache's transfer, more than two disks; NFC and NFD are one pair of names among others, the drivers telling them apart as the model's exact driver does.

## Conflicts and their resolution: Conflicts.tla

A model of its own, `Conflicts.tla` with `MCConflicts.tla` (2026-11-25), of a file in conflict and its resolution (§15.7), and of the repair of a corrupt copy that changed (§16.1). One file, a copy on each of two disks, each copy an object holding a version, as `Replication.tla` has them: the file in conflict from the start, as after concurrent changes, at a disk's adoption or after `catalog rebuild`, or corrupt, disk 1's copy rotted and disk 2's healthy. The catalog's file, the original it presents and each copy's row; Atoll's own view, which presents the copy the catalog names (`view.presentFile`: the original of a file in conflict, the copy it presented before) and a handle a program holds open on it to write; `atoll conflicts resolve --keep`, step by step (`trash.Session.Resolve`): its checks, its look at each copy as it is now, its event before anything moves, the kept copy's row brought to what its disk holds, then each other copy in turn, left on a disk that cannot be reached, its row gone when it is no longer there, otherwise its row brought to what its disk holds and sent to the trash, `overwritten`, the move checked against that row right before it (`trash.Session.Send`); once none is left, the file pending from the kept copy, which the next pass copies to the other disk. The repair, by chunk: a copy that changed since it was found corrupt settled before anything is written, a conflict when it holds another version; otherwise the bad chunks written from the healthy copy, the copy compared with its row right before the first write. A user writes in place, saves atomically or deletes, on either disk at any moment; a disk is lost and mounted again.

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `NoVersionLost` | §15.7, every version kept: no version a user wrote is lost, but what the user's own write, save or deletion destroyed, until a resolve the user asked for, and then only one that resolve said it sends away, to the trash |
| `OnlyWhatItSays` | §15.7: the resolve removes only what it says, and only through the trash |
| `ViewNeverSentAway` | §15.7, §21.2: the view never presents a version a resolve sent away, nor opens one |

Measured on 2026-11-25 with `test/tla/run.sh`, 2 workers, in the run of the whole set (above):

| Instance | Expects | What it explores | States | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `Conflicts` | pass | a file in conflict, version 1 on disk 1, presented, version 2 on disk 2: a user writing, saving or deleting on either disk three times, a program writing through the view, a disk lost once, and `atoll conflicts resolve --keep` either disk | 2,677,679 | 23 s |
| `ConflictsCorrupt` | pass | disk 1's copy rotted, disk 2's healthy, presented: a user writing or saving three times, a program writing through the view, a disk lost once, the repair by chunk, then `atoll conflicts resolve` | 566,224 | 6 s |
| `FlawConflictViewWrite` | pass | flaw 26, fixed: a program writing through the view during `atoll conflicts resolve` | 141 | 1 s |

### Flaw 26: a write opened through the view on a copy a resolve sends to the trash goes there

Found on 2026-11-25 by `NoVersionLost`; `FlawConflictViewWrite.cfg` (60 states). Fixed on 2026-11-25 with the maintainer's remedy, `resolve-view-free` (The remedy, below).

§15.7: "every version kept", the resolve sending "every other copy to the trash"; §21.2, as flaw 10's remedy has it for the SSD cache's transfer: a write through the view is never in a copy Atoll moves to the trash.

1. A file in conflict, version 1 on disk 1, which the view presents, version 2 on disk 2.
2. A program opens it through the view to write: the view opens disk 1's copy.
3. `atoll conflicts resolve --keep 2` looks at each copy, writes its event, sends disk 1's copy to the trash, its move checked against its row, which the program has not written yet, and ends the conflict.
4. The program writes, into the object it holds open, now in disk 1's trash: a version no resolve said it sends away, on no path of the pool.

`trash.Session.Send`, which the resolve uses, journals its move without asking the view's journal (`TrashStart.ViewFree` false), which the SSD cache's transfer asks since flaw 10's remedy. `TestFlawAWriteOpenedThroughTheViewDuringAResolveIsLost` (build tag `flaws`, then; now `TestAWriteOpenedThroughTheViewDuringAResolveIsKept`) ran it in the code: the write was in `t1:Atoll/trash/…/a.mkv` alone.

Remedy proposed, checked by TLC on 2026-11-25: `resolve-view-free`, the resolve's move of each other copy waiting, as the transfer's does, while an operation of the view no pass has taken in touches its path, a write held open among them: that copy stays, as one that changed between the look and the move does, and so does the conflict, until a run after the program closed it. It holds in every instance, and in the two pools with a program writing through the view, in copies not kept (`Conflicts` 2,677,679 states with the view, `ConflictsCorrupt` 566,224); without it, `NoVersionLost` breaks there (`Conflicts` 3,535, `ConflictsCorrupt` 11,030, the same trace). In the code, `ViewFree: true` for the resolve's move, its outcome `view-holds` reported as `changed`, which `api/schemas/conflicts-resolve.schema.json` already lists for a copy that changed between the look and the move, and counted as a copy that stays: a few lines in `internal/trash`, no contract change; an outcome of its own would be one (the maintainer's). For a user: a resolve while a program writes the file through the view leaves that copy where it is, and the conflict, until it is run again.

### The remedy of flaw 26

The maintainer chose `resolve-view-free` on 2026-11-25, reporting `changed`, no new outcome (DONE.md): `atoll conflicts resolve` moves no copy the view holds open; it reports that copy `changed`, and the conflict stays until a run that can move it.

**Held open, not untaken.** The remedy proposed had the resolve wait as the transfer does, while an operation of the view no pass has taken in touches the path (`TrashStart.ViewFree`). In the code it never ends: no pass takes in a write to a file in conflict, as the walk leaves its copies as it found them, so a program's write through the view, closed long ago, held every resolve of that file, `changed` at each run (`TestAWriteOpenedThroughTheViewDuringAResolveIsKept`, seen so). The model's remedy is the narrower one, what the maintainer's words say: the move stays while the view holds that very copy open (`vh.o = s`). So the code reads, in the transaction that journals the move, a write the view holds open on that copy, `open` on its disk, or an operation of the view not finished at, below or above its path, `planned` on any (`TrashStart.HeldFree`, `catalog.viewHolds`); an operation done waits for nothing more, the resolve having looked at the copy with it, and the move compared with that look. A held write that opens after the move is journaled waits for it already (`catalog.BeginViewOp`, flaw 10).

**The write done goes with its copy.** Once a resolve ended the conflict, a write the view made and finished into a copy it sent away stayed open in the view's journal, and the view presented that copy, gone: nothing at the file's path, until no pass would take it in (`TestAWriteOpenedThroughTheViewDuringAResolveIsKept`, seen so). The end of the conflict takes it in (`catalog.ResolveConflict`): the version it wrote is the one the resolve looked at and sent, as the resolve said, and the view presents the copy kept.

In the code: `trash.Session.resolveOther` sends with `HeldFree` and reports `view-holds` as `changed`, a copy that stays (exit 4); `catalog.TrashStart.HeldFree`, `viewHolds`, and the take-in in `ResolveConflict`. No contract change: `changed` is in `api/schemas/conflicts-resolve.schema.json` already. specs.md says it in §15.7. The tests: `TestAWriteOpenedThroughTheViewDuringAResolveIsKept` (`internal/jobs/resolveview_test.go`), and `TestAResolveLeavesWhatAProgramWritesThroughTheView` through the view served by FUSE on ext4 and ntfs-3g, on Linux 6.12 and 7.3. In the model, the remedy is what every instance does, `Fixes` is empty, `Conflicts` and `ConflictsCorrupt` have a program writing through the view, and `FlawConflictViewWrite` expects pass. For a user: a resolve while a program writes the file through the view leaves that copy where it is, `changed`, and the conflict, with exit 4, until it is run again once the program closed it; then it sends that copy as it says, the program's write in it, and the view presents the copy kept.

### Modelling mistakes, found by TLC and fixed

- A program's write through the view into a file a user's own save had replaced counted as a version lost: what a program writes into a file the user's own save or deletion took from its name is that user's to lose.
- `ViewNeverSentAway` read the row of the copy the view presents, which a user's save leaves pointing at the object it replaced: it now asks only that the object is in no trash.

### What it leaves out

The passes but their copy (`Replication.tla`'s), the scrub's other steps and the repair by file, the chunk's bytes (a version stands for the content), more copies and disks, the steps of a move and their reprise, the power cut, `atoll conflicts list`.

## The safety guards: Guards.tla

A model of its own, `Guards.tla` with `MCGuards.tla` (2026-11-25), of the safety guards of §15.6, the first of the maintainer's models by risk (TODO.md): the mass-deletion guard and the mass-modification guard of a disk, with its day, `modifies.day_guard`. Two disks: D, where a user deletes, puts back and rewrites files directly, whose guards are modelled, and E, which holds the other copy of each file, where a user may edit one. Time is a pass's: each walk is a tick, and an answer comes between two passes, as it takes the pool's lock that a pass holds. A pass walks D, recording a copy found gone, or back as it was; works out the mass-deletion guard once (`catalog.ReadGuard`, `jobs.readGuards`: the gone rows past the last answer's `up_to`, and the `copy-gone` events within the window, against `files`), writing a `guard` event when it trips with none standing and a `guard-clear` when it no longer trips with one standing; reads the mass-modification guard (`jobs.readModifyGuards`: it stands while a `modify-guard` event stands since its last answer); decides file by file, a gone copy held while the guard is tripped and its gone time is past the answer's `up_to`, a deletion when E's copy is what its row says, a kept file otherwise, and a copy changed on D asked of its guard (`jobs.guardModification`: a change made by the `up_to` of any reject is a conflict, `catalog.ModifyGuardRead.Rejected`; an answer that covers its change time and has not lapsed decides it, a reject as a conflict; otherwise the counts over the window then over the day, the decision that would trip either held, with its event); then runs its trash jobs one at a time, each held while the mass-deletion guard of its `src_disk` is tripped, a deletion dropped when the walk found D's copy back, each move checked against the copy's row. `atoll guard confirm` and `reject`, of either kind, are one action each, the CLI's and the local API's being the same code (`catalog.ConfirmGuard`, `RejectGuard`, `AnswerModifyGuard`). The daemon may stop at any step, and the next pass starts again from what the catalog holds; D may be unmounted, its mount point empty, and mounted again.

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `DelBounded` | §15.6: the deletions decided without an answer within the mass-deletion guard's window are at most its `files` |
| `ModBounded`, `DayBounded` | §15.6: the modifications decided without an answer within the mass-modification guard's window, and within its day, since the last answer to a hold of each, are at most their `files` |
| `NoTrashHeld` | §15.6: no copy goes to E's trash for a deletion the mass-deletion guard holds, unanswered |
| `NoTrashRefused` | §15.6: nor ever for a deletion a reject answered |
| `NoOverwriteHeld` | §15.6: no copy of E is overwritten for a change the mass-modification guard holds, unanswered |
| `NoOverwriteRefused` | §15.6: nor ever for a change a reject answered |
| `EventuallySettled` | liveness: once the user stops acting and answers each guard that asks, the passes settle: nothing gone left undecided, no job, no guard standing, every change on D decided |

What a guard asks about is a ghost, a status of each deletion by its gone time and of each change by its change time: asked once a hold counts it, allowed or refused by the answer whose `up_to` covers it, and free again when the answer leaves it out, past its `up_to`, as it then counts anew and is asked again only if it trips the guard again. A hold that ends by itself, with a `guard-clear`, allows what it held: below the threshold, a deletion goes unasked, as §15.6 says. A missing disk is not walked, so nothing of it is ever found gone (invariant 4), whatever the user did meanwhile.

Measured on 2026-11-25 with `test/tla/run.sh`, 2 workers, once flaw 27's remedy was in the model; before it, `GuardsMod` explored 3,690,255 states and `GuardsBoth` 162,988, each without `NoOverwriteRefused`, and the flaw's two instances found it in 7,570 and 24,048:

| Instance | Expects | What it explores | States | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `GuardsDel` | pass | two files on D and E, the mass-deletion guard tripping past one; a user deleting, putting back, editing on E, unmounting D, or the daemon stopping, four times in all; confirm and reject | 287,304 | 5 s |
| `GuardsMod` | pass | two files, the mass-modification guard tripping past one within a pass, its day past one within three; a user rewriting on D, editing on E, unmounting D, or the daemon stopping, four times in all; confirm and reject | 3,658,469 | 35 s |
| `GuardsBoth` | pass | both guards at once: a user deleting, putting back, rewriting on D, editing on E, or the daemon stopping, three times in all; every answer | 162,930 | 4 s |
| `GuardsLive` | pass | `EventuallySettled`: both guards, a user deleting, putting back, rewriting, unmounting D, or the daemon stopping, three times in all, every guard answered | 54,155 | 3 s |
| `FlawGuardRejectLapsed` | pass | flaw 27, fixed: a reject that lapses before the pass that decides what it covered | 41,517 | 2 s |
| `FlawGuardRejectSuperseded` | pass | flaw 27, fixed: a later hold confirmed before the pass that decides what a reject covered | 352,449 | 5 s |

`GuardsLive` checks `EventuallySettled` under `LiveSpec`: each step of a pass weakly fair, a pass ending once its jobs ran or wait for the guard, D mounted again, and the answers strongly fair, as an answer waits for the lock that each pass takes and gets it between two. The daemon's stops count among the user's actions, so they end.

### Flaw 27: a reject of the mass-modification guard lapses, or is superseded, before a pass decided what it covered

Found on 2026-11-25 by `NoOverwriteRefused`, and fixed that day with the remedy the maintainer chose, `reject-kept` (below, The remedy of flaw 27).

§15.6: a reject makes a modified decision "a `conflict` instead (§15.7): every version stays, and nothing is overwritten"; "An answer holds for `window` after it is given ... A pass that starts past it finds no answer. What the answer covered is then a change like any other: it counts, and the guard asks again ... once the count trips it."

An answer of the mass-modification guard is read as the guard's last answer, within its window (`catalog.ModifyGuardRead.Answers`), at each pass. A reject is not recorded on the files it covered: only a pass that decides each of them while it is the last answer, and has not lapsed, makes it a conflict. A change it covered that no such pass decided is then a change like any other, which counts; below the threshold it is decided as a modification, and the other copies are overwritten, the version the user kept by rejecting going to the trash, where its retention or `trash.max_size` purges it. Two ways, each an instance:

- `FlawGuardRejectLapsed` (7,570 states): a and b rewritten on D. A pass decides a, and b would trip the guard: held, its event. The user rejects. The pass after it stops before b is decided; the next starts past the window: the reject lapsed, b counts as one change, under the threshold, and is decided: E's copy of b is overwritten. A daemon down longer than the window after a reject does the same, with no stop at all.
- `FlawGuardRejectSuperseded` (24,048 states): a decided, b held and rejected. In the pass after it, a later change of a trips the day before b is decided, and the daemon stops. The user confirms that new hold, whose `up_to`, its own event's time, covers b's change too: the confirm decides b as a modification.

`TestFlawARejectedChangeOverwritesTheOtherCopies` (build tag `flaws`, then `internal/jobs/flaws_test.go`, now `TestARejectedChangeStaysAConflict`) ran both in the code: the first with the daemon down past the window, the second with the rejected file unreadable in the pass where the later change trips the day, as a pass stopped there leaves it; t2's copy, which the reject kept, was overwritten in each. `TestAnAnswerToTheModifyGuardLapses` pinned the lapse of a reject as §15.6 said it, which is the flaw's first way.

Remedies proposed, each an entry of `Fixes` then, checked by TLC on 2026-11-25 against every invariant, in every instance of the module, in copies not kept; the maintainer chose (a) that day:

- (a) `reject-kept`: a change made by the `up_to` of any reject of that disk's guard is a conflict, at any later pass, whatever answer came after it, however long ago the reject was given: what a reject covered is never decided otherwise. It holds in every instance (`GuardsMod` 3,658,469 states, `GuardsBoth` 162,930, the two flaw instances 41,517 and 352,449, `GuardsLive` 54,155 with its liveness). A conflict overwrites nothing, so a reject that stands for good loses nothing; a change made after its `up_to` is another, which counts. In the code, `ReadModifyGuard` reads the latest `up_to` of the disk's `modify-guard-reject` events besides the last answer, and `guardModification` makes a change at or before it a conflict before anything else; `heldBefore` lets such a change through to that, as an answer does: a query on the events by kind, as the others, a few lines; no contract change. For a user: a reject keeps every version of what it covered for good, whatever comes after it; the guard no longer asks again about a rejected change once its window passed, which, a conflict, `atoll conflicts resolve` then settles.
- (b) `reject-stands`: a reject never lapses, while still read only as the last answer. It closes the first way, not the second: `NoOverwriteRefused` in `FlawGuardRejectSuperseded` (90,307 states) and `GuardsMod` (642,011).
- (c) `lapse-asks`: a change a lapsed answer covered is held and asked about again, whatever the count. It closes the first way, not the second either: `NoOverwriteRefused` in `FlawGuardRejectSuperseded` (93,364) and `GuardsMod` (649,198); with (b), the same (89,922).

The mass-deletion guard has no such flaw: a reject removes the gone rows it covers in its own transaction (`catalog.RejectGuard`), so nothing is left for a later pass or answer to decide otherwise. A confirm is applied by the next pass, so a later reject before it may undo it, removing every queued deletion of that disk: what it sends back is a copy kept, never one lost, and `atoll guard list` shows those jobs among what the reject answers.

### The remedy of flaw 27

`reject-kept` (the maintainer, 2026-11-25). A change made by the `up_to` of any reject of the mass-modification guard of its disk is a conflict at every later pass, whatever answer came after it, however long ago the reject was given, and nothing holds it. In the code, `catalog.ReadModifyGuard` reads the greatest `up_to` of the disk's `modify-guard-reject` events (`RejectUpTo`, through the index of the events by kind, `TestGuardReadsUseAnIndex`), the last one's but for a clock set back, which `TestTheRejectsOfTheModifyGuardAreKept` pins; `ModifyGuardRead.Rejected` says whether it covers a change time; `jobs.guardModification` makes such a change a conflict before anything else, and `heldBefore` lets it through to that, as an answer does. No contract change: `conflict` is a decision already, and neither `atoll guard list` nor `status` says anything new; `atoll guard reject --kind modifications` with no daemon to tell no longer says on standard error how long a pass has to start, which a confirm still says (`tellOfAnswer`). specs.md says it in §15.6: a reject keeps what it covered for good, a confirm still lapses past its window, and a `catalog rebuild`, which loses every event, rejects included (§9), makes copies with different hashes a conflict, so what a reject kept stays kept.

The tests (`internal/jobs/rejectkept_test.go`), each seen failing before the remedy but one, which passed before too: `TestARejectedChangeStaysAConflict`, the flaw's two ways, the daemon down past the window and a later confirm that came first, the rejected file unreadable in that pass; `TestAStopAfterAReject`, a stop as by kill -9 at the commit of another change before the rejected one is decided, at the commit of its conflict (which passed before too: the conflict was recorded already), and the rejected file unreadable, then the reject lapsed; `TestSeveralRejects`, two rejects, each keeping what it covered, and a change made since that counts; `TestARejectSurvivesARestart`, a restart past the window, the reject read again from the catalog alone. `TestAnAnswerToTheModifyGuardLapses` keeps the lapse of a confirm, as before, and no longer runs a reject; `TestARejectOfTheModifyGuardDoesNotLapse` pins what a reject does past its window now: every change it covered a conflict, nothing held, nothing counted, a change made since decided as any other. The scenarios: `daemon-modify-guard-reject-kept.json`, the daemon down past the reject's window, which fails before the remedy (exit 3, a rejected file modified and the next held again); and `daemon-modify-guard-reject-rebuild.json`, a `catalog rebuild` between the reject and the next pass, which passes before and after: the rebuild loses the reject, and its own rule makes the files it covered conflicts. In the model, the remedy is what every instance does, `Fixes` is empty, `GuardsMod` and `GuardsBoth` check `NoOverwriteRefused` too, and the flaw's two instances expect `pass`. For a user: a reject keeps every version of what it covered for good, whatever comes after it, until `atoll conflicts resolve` settles each; the guard no longer asks again about a rejected change once the reject's window passed.

### How sensitive it is

Each mechanism taken out, in a copy of the module not kept: the hold of a gone copy, `DelBounded` in `GuardsDel` (5,792 states); the guard's trip itself, the same (5,731); the gone rows a reject removes, `NoTrashRefused` (16,388); the hold of a standing `modify-guard`, `NoOverwriteHeld` in `GuardsMod` (151,319); the circuit breaker, `ModBounded` (3,464); the day, `DayBounded` (18,988). The wait of the trash jobs while the guard is tripped changes nothing alone, the hold of the gone rows keeping what it would; without both, `NoTrashHeld` (5,113). In the model, the jobs it holds are those decided before the guard tripped, or confirmed: none was asked about, so no invariant here needs it, though a reject removes them, as `atoll guard list` shows them.

### Modelling mistakes, found by TLC and fixed

- What a hold held past its answer's `up_to` stayed asked, so a change made after a confirm's `up_to`, decided under the threshold once the hold was answered, read as overwritten while held: what an answer leaves out counts anew, and is asked again only if it trips the guard again.
- `EventuallySettled` failed with the answers weakly fair: enabled only between two passes, an answer could wait for ever as the passes went on; it waits for the pool's lock, which it gets between two, so the answers are strongly fair.

### What it leaves out

The percent of each guard, its `files` alone; refused renames (§15.3) and what a copy found moved hides from the mass-deletion guard; the guards of E, whose edits are decided without them; the link guard, the view's deletions and writes, the SSD cache's transfers and the copies at once, each of which reaches the guards only through the rows and events modelled here (`Replication.tla`'s, `Copies.tla`'s); the slices of reads of a pass that reads for hours (`takenUnder`); the copies a plan makes again; `atoll guard list` and dry runs, which read only.

## The trash: Trash.tla

A model of its own, `Trash.tla` with `MCTrash.tla` (2026-11-25), of §17, §17.1 to §17.4, the purges at once of §19 and §21.2, and invariants 1, 2, 10, 11 and 12: the second of the maintainer's models by risk (TODO.md), and the only place where Atoll destroys data for good. Two disks, each holding a copy of each file at the start, at the same path. A file's data is an object, with its inode number, which its filesystem gives again once no name leads to it, and which may have several names: its path in the root, an entry's path in the trash, and, with the link guard, its link in `links/`. Time is the commands': a tick between two of them. Atoll is one process, each command under the pool's lock, each starting with the reprise of what the journal holds planned on each disk its session can write to (§17.2), a disk lost during a session lost for the rest of it:

- a pass, simplified: each file decided from what its disks hold, a deletion propagated through the trash to the other copy unchanged since in sync (`deleted`), a modification making the other copy stale and refreshing it through the trash (`overwritten`), a copy the catalog has not recorded taken in or its file made again; then its jobs, each move to the trash journaled with its trash row, the copy compared with its row right before the move, then the commit (§17.1); a copy, one step (`Replication.tla`'s); then, with the link guard, each disk's links decided from one read (`solver.PlanLinks`) and changed one at a time, kept, removed by `ops.DropLink` only when the data has another name, sent to the trash from the link, `deleted` or `overwritten`, or made (§17);
- `atoll prune` (§17.3), the copy that stays looked at, then the surplus copy to the trash (`trash.Session.Prune`); with `atonce`, purged from there at once once the other disk's copy is looked at again, as the SSD cache's transfer (§19) and the view's move (§21.2) purge theirs; with `inplace`, removed at its path after that look, as the view's move does when the trash has no room; and `atoll balance` (§13.3), its old copy to the trash the same way, as `rebalance` (`trash.Session.Retire`);
- `atoll trash restore` (§17.4), the newest entry of a path, the path looked at, the entry moved back without replacing anything (`ops.FromTrash`), the commit;
- a purge (§17, §17.4): what reached its expiry; with `room`, what goes for room too, `surplus`, `rebalance` and `overwritten`, as the trash may hold more than `trash.max_size` at any time, a `surplus` or `rebalance` entry only once a look finds its version on the other disk (`trash.Session.purgeFor`); with `older`, anything, as `--older-than` does, the user's choice; never an entry the same run's reprise sent there; for each, the links of the guard that lead to it removed first (`ops.OwnLinksOf`, each an operation of its own), then the operation `unlink` journaled, the name removed if it holds the entry's inode (`ops.PurgeEntry`), the commit (`catalog.FinishPurge`).

A user acts directly on a disk at any moment: deletes, replaces by an atomic save, edits in place or makes a copy; edits, removes or takes back by hand an entry of the trash; unmounts a disk or mounts it read-only; and Atoll's process may be killed between any two steps.

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `NoKeptLost` | §17, invariants 1 and 10: no step of Atoll's takes the last of a version it must keep: the one a user has at a path; one a user deleted while the pool held it elsewhere, for the retention, as its other copies then go to the trash, deleted, or with the guard its link; an entry deleted within its retention, as a user left it. Expired entries, and what goes for room, are the trash's to purge, and what `--older-than` purges the user's choice |
| `RestoreExact` | §17.4: a restore that finishes puts back the very file its entry holds, as a user left it there, or nothing |
| `OneAtATime` | §17.2: never a purge of an entry while a restore of it, or another purge of it, is planned |
| `JournaledFirst` | invariant 12: every name Atoll takes away, from the root, a trash or a links folder, is taken by a step of an operation planned in the journal |
| `PurgedStaysPurged` | §17, A purge: what a purge took from the trash keeps no link of the guard, which would keep its data once purged and send it back to the trash at the next pass |
| `PurgeSettles` | liveness: an operation of a purge, a removal at its path or a link's removal never stays planned, Atoll fair and a disk coming back |

What Atoll must keep is a ghost of the user's: the version a user has at each path, set by what they write and by a restore once the entry reaches the path, cleared by a deletion; and the versions a user deleted while the pool still held them elsewhere, each for the retention. A deletion here is the removal of the version the user has with no other version left at the file's path: one of an old copy beside the version the user has is a replacement's, a modification (invariant 10), and a mix of versions the user wrote on the two disks leaves no claim on the file, what the passes make of it being `Replication.tla`'s. What a user's own action takes the last of, and what `--older-than` purges, is theirs to lose.

Measured on 2026-11-26 with `test/tla/run.sh`, 2 workers, once flaw 28's remedy was in the model; before it, the flaw's two instances found it in 5,765 and 46,928 states, and `TrashPrune`, prune alone, explored 104,880:

| Instance | Expects | What it explores | States | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `TrashPurge` | pass | one file on two disks; a user deleting, replacing or editing a copy, unmounting a disk, or Atoll killed, three times in all; the purge by expiry and for room | 1,002,630 | 17 s |
| `TrashRestore` | pass | `atoll trash restore` and the purge by expiry; a user deleting a copy, making a file at the path, editing, removing or taking back an entry of the trash by hand, unmounting a disk, or Atoll killed, three times in all | 84,148 | 3 s |
| `TrashGuard` | pass | the link guard: a user deleting or replacing a copy around Atoll, or Atoll killed, three times in all; the purge for room | 817,450 | 17 s |
| `TrashGuardRestore` | pass | the link guard with restores, the purge for room and `--older-than`; a user deleting or replacing a copy, editing, removing or taking back an entry by hand, or Atoll killed, three times in all | 4,360,072 | 1 min 20 s |
| `TrashDisks` | pass | a disk lost or mounted read-only at any step: the link guard, restores and purges; a user deleting a copy, unmounting a disk, mounting one read-only, or Atoll killed, three times in all | 2,424,934 | 44 s |
| `TrashInodes` | pass | two files, an inode given again: the link guard and the purge by expiry; a user deleting a copy, making a file, removing an entry by hand, or Atoll killed, three times in all | 463,266 | 13 s |
| `TrashPrune` | pass | `atoll prune` and `atoll balance` with the link guard, disk 1's copy surplus; a user deleting or editing a copy, or Atoll killed, twice in all; the purge for room | 208,232 | 5 s |
| `TrashAtOnce` | pass | the purge at once of the transfer and the view's move, with the link guard; a user deleting or editing a copy, unmounting a disk, or Atoll killed, three times in all; the purge for room | 2,376,889 | 37 s |
| `TrashInPlace` | pass | the removal at its path after its look, the guard off; a user deleting or editing a copy, unmounting a disk, or Atoll killed, three times in all; the purge by expiry | 286,352 | 7 s |
| `TrashLive` | pass | `PurgeSettles`: the link guard, restores, the purge for room; a user deleting or replacing a copy, unmounting a disk, or Atoll killed, twice in all | 205,897 | 13 s |
| `FlawTrashSurplusPruned` | pass | flaw 28, fixed: prune, the guard off | 35,692 | 2 s |
| `FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce` | pass | flaw 28, fixed: the purge at once of the transfer, the guard off | 593,031 | 12 s |
| `FlawTrashRebalanced` | pass | flaw 28, fixed: balance, the guard off | 35,692 | 2 s |

`TrashLive` checks `PurgeSettles` under `LiveSpec`: each of Atoll's steps weakly fair, a disk coming back. A journal of the instance full ends the command that would journal, as a stop does: the bound of the model, not of Atoll.

### Flaw 28: a surplus copy sent to the trash while a user deletes the copy that stays is purged for room before a deletion's retention

Found on 2026-11-25 by `NoKeptLost`, and fixed on 2026-11-26 with the remedy the maintainer chose, `surplus-looked`, which he extended to `rebalance` that day (below, The remedy of flaw 28).

§15.5 and §17: a deletion propagates to the other copies "only to the trash", and "Deleted and renamed files are never purged before their retention ends"; a purge for room takes "surplus and rebalance" first; §17.3: `atoll prune` looks at "each copy of each file that has a `surplus` one", then sends the surplus copy to the trash, `surplus`.

Prune looks at the copies that stay once, before the move of the surplus copy (`trash.Session.decidePrune`), and the move compares only the copy it moves with its row (§17.1, step 4). A user who deletes the copies that stay directly on their disks, after that look and before the move, around Atoll, with the link guard off, deletes the file, and its last data is then the surplus copy, which reaches the trash as `surplus`. Had prune not run, the deletion would have reached that copy as `deleted`, kept for the whole retention; as `surplus`, a purge for room takes it first, at once when the trash holds more than `trash.max_size`. Two ways, each an instance:

- `FlawTrashSurplusPruned` (5,765 states): disk 1's copy surplus, the guard off. Prune looks at disk 2's and journals the move of disk 1's; the user deletes disk 2's directly; the move goes, `surplus`; a purge for room takes it, the deleted file's last data, before the retention its deletion gives it.
- `FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce` (46,928 states): the same through the purge at once of the SSD cache's transfer: stopped before its look, or its look finding disk 2's copy gone (flaw 8's remedy), it leaves the entry in the trash, `surplus`, for a purge for room.

With the link guard on, it holds (`TrashPrune`, `TrashAtOnce`): the link of the copy deleted around Atoll goes to the trash as `deleted`. `atoll balance` sends its old copy by the same path as prune, `trash.Session.Retire`, one look at the copies that stay then the move, and `TestFlawARebalancedCopySentAsTheOthersGoIsPurgedForRoom` (2026-11-26) showed the same way in the code, its entry `rebalance`, which a purge for room takes with `surplus`, purged `max-size`; the model has it since the fix (`FlawTrashRebalanced`).

`TestFlawASurplusCopySentAsTheOthersGoIsPurgedForRoom` (build tag `flaws`, then `internal/trash/flaws_test.go`, now `TestASurplusCopySentAsTheOthersGoStaysForItsRetention`) ran the first way in the code: a on three disks, t3's copy surplus; at prune's checkpoint `journaled` the copies of t1 and t2 are deleted; t3's reaches the trash as `surplus`, and a purge with the trash past `trash.max_size` purges it, `max-size`. No black-box scenario: the runner cannot act between prune's look and its move, and a crash there is undone by the reprise.

Remedies proposed, each an entry of `Fixes` then, checked by TLC on 2026-11-25 against every invariant, in every instance of the module, in copies not kept; the maintainer chose (a) on 2026-11-26, and extended it to `rebalance` that day:

- (a) `surplus-looked`: a purge for room takes a `surplus` entry before its retention ends only once a look at the file's other copies, as §15.10 compares a known copy, finds its version at the file's path on another disk, as that copy's row says, as the transfer's purge at once looks since flaw 8; otherwise the entry waits for its retention, as a deleted one does. It holds in every instance (FlawTrashSurplusPruned 35,692 states, FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce 593,031, TrashPrune 100,953, TrashAtOnce 2,376,889, TrashLive 205,897 with its liveness). In the code, the purge for room looks at the other copies of each `surplus` entry it would take, which wakes those disks; the choice of `solver.ChoosePurge` stays, an entry that fails the look staying. For a user: what a deletion leaves only in a surplus entry stays for the retention; a purge for room may give back less room, `full` saying so.
- (b) `surplus-retained`: a `surplus` entry never goes for room, only at the end of its retention or with `--older-than`; the purges at once, after their look, stay. It holds in every instance (FlawTrashSurplusPruned 29,303 states, FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce 577,800, TrashPrune 89,111, TrashAtOnce 2,334,452). In the code, `surplus` leaves the ranks of `roomCandidates`, with `rebalance` if it goes the same way, and `conformance/vectors/trash_purge.json` changes. For a user: a pruned copy holds its room in the trash for the whole retention, 30 days by default, and the trash past `trash.max_size` has only overwritten, repaired and quarantined entries to give back; for the SSD cache, an entry a stop or a failed look leaves holds its room on the SSD until then.
- (c) `prune-relook`: prune looks again at the copy that stays right before the move, once it is journaled. It closes neither way: `NoKeptLost` in `FlawTrashSurplusPruned` (7,639 states) and `FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce` (55,175), a deletion that comes after the look.

### The remedy of flaw 28

`surplus-looked` (the maintainer, 2026-11-26), with `rebalance`: a purge for room takes a `surplus` or `rebalance` entry before its retention ends only while another disk holds its version. In the code, in two steps, so that a purge looks only at what it is about to take. The choice first (`solver.ChoosePurge`, pure): an entry marked `Unbacked` never goes for room, and the next goes in its place; the caller asks the catalog of each `surplus` or `rebalance` entry the choice would take for room whether a copy of its file at its path on another disk is `ok`, not found gone, with the entry's hash (`catalog.Backed`, through the index of the files' paths, `TestBackedIsAskedOfOneFile`), and makes the choice again without the ones no copy backs (`trash.chooseForRoom`, for the command and the daemon's slices, `PurgeDue` and the full-trash alert alike). Then the look (`trash.Session.purgeFor`, `heldElsewhere`): right before such an entry is purged for room, those copies are looked at as §15.10 compares a known copy, on the disks the session can read, `readonly` ones included; none found as its row says, the entry stays, `changed`, as the transfer's purge leaves its copy since flaw 8, and the command exits 4; a disk that fails the look is lost (invariant 4). The purge by age and `--older-than` look at nothing, nor does a run without `--yes`. `conformance/vectors/trash_purge.json` has the field `unbacked`, optional, and six cases more; no contract change: `changed` is an outcome of `trash-purge.schema.json` already. specs.md says it in §17, §17.3, §13.3 and §19.

What it costs: the look wakes the disks of the copies that back an entry, for a purge for room alone, and once per entry the choice would take, a stat of each copy; until a walk records a copy a user deleted, the catalog still backs the entry, and each purge for room looks at it again, then the entry is no longer chosen and nothing is looked at. A surplus or rebalance entry whose file has changed since, or whose other copies went, waits for its retention, and the trash may stay past `trash.max_size` meanwhile, `full` and the alert saying so.

The tests (`internal/trash/surpluslook_test.go`), each seen failing before the remedy but those of a look that finds the copy: `TestASurplusCopySentAsTheOthersGoStaysForItsRetention` (prune, then a scan that knows the copies gone, then the retention), `TestARebalancedCopySentAsTheOthersGoStaysForItsRetention` (balance's `Retire`), `TestASurplusEntryGoesForRoomWhenACopyHoldsIt`, `TestTheLookBeforeAPurgeForRoom` (`EIO` on both copies, the disks lost; on one, the other holding; both disks missing; both read-only; a stop as by kill -9 at the look and right after it, nothing journaled, the next run looking again), `TestAPurgeByAgeLooksAtNothing`, `TestATransfersEntryStaysForItsRetention` (the transfer's look finding the data copies gone, and a stop before its look, the reprise finishing the move); `TestAPurgeForRoomLooksFirst` on each driver, on Linux 6.12 and 7.3; the scenario `trash-purge-max-size-surplus-looked.json`, which fails before the remedy, and `trash-purge-max-size-crash-looked.json`, a stop at the point `looked`, nothing journaled, the next purge looking again. In the model, the remedy is what every instance does, `Fixes` is empty, `rebalance` a reason the purge for room ranks with `surplus`, with an `atoll balance` that sends it as prune sends its own, and the flaw's two instances and `FlawTrashRebalanced` expect `pass`. For a user: what a deletion leaves only in a pruned, balanced or transferred copy stays for the retention; a purge for room may give back less room than before, and wakes the disks that hold the other copies.

### Observations

- A restore takes its entry by its inode, as an entry records no modification time and nothing but Atoll writes in its trash (§17.2): an entry a user edited by hand goes back as they left it, and one they removed gives `changed`. `RestoreExact` holds of the object; what a user wrote into the trash is theirs.
- The look before a purge at once and before a removal at its path, and the restore's move checked against the entry's inode, change nothing here when taken out: what they guard against is not in this model, the catalog's word made stale through the view or a disk failing the look (`Replication.tla`, flaw 8), a file someone else put at an entry's name.

### How sensitive it is

Each mechanism taken out, in a copy of the module not kept: the look before a purge for room, `NoKeptLost` in `FlawTrashSurplusPruned` (5,664 states), `FlawTrashSurplusAtOnce` (46,531) and `FlawTrashRebalanced` (5,667); `rebalance` left out of it, the same in `FlawTrashRebalanced` (5,764); a purge for room that takes `deleted` entries too, `NoKeptLost` in `TrashPurge` (61,934 states); the move compared with its row right before it, the same (76,971); `ops.DropLink`'s refusal of a link that is the last name of its data, the same in `TrashInodes` (35,645) and `TrashGuardRestore` (29,802); the links that lead to an entry dropped before its purge, at once or not, `PurgedStaysPurged` in `TrashGuard` (11,566), `TrashGuardRestore` (19,685) and `TrashAtOnce` (17,562); the session's disks as its mount checks found them at its start, `OneAtATime` in `TrashDisks` (61,126) and `TrashPurge` (180,694); the reprise first in every command, the same in `TrashGuardRestore` (22,683) and `TrashDisks` (40,290); the reprise of a purge, `PurgeSettles` in `TrashLive` (35,710); the purge journaled first, `JournaledFirst` in `TrashPurge` (62,761). Some change nothing here, each for a reason: a disk lost during a session and back within it, which a session that went on with it would find as it left it, its planned operations its own; the hold of what a run's own reprise sent to the trash, and the retention that reprise starts again, which are about what a user could see of an entry, and lose nothing; and the looks and the inode check of the observations above.

### Modelling mistakes, found by TLC and fixed

- A disk that came back during a command was used again by it without its reprise: a session uses a disk only as its mount check found it at its start, as the device id is checked again before each operation (§17.1).
- A deletion's queued trash job ran after a later pass decided a modification of the file, and sent the user's new version to the trash, deleted: a new decision of a file drops its queued jobs, as the code and `Replication.tla` do.
- The catalog had no `stale` state, so a stale copy a user removed read as a deletion of the current one: the other copies of a modified file are stale until refreshed, and a stale copy gone is made again.
- The ghost kept a deletion's promise after the user wrote the file again, read the removal of an old copy beside the version the user has as a deletion, and counted as holding a deleted version a link whose data is an entry of the trash, or whose copy the user replaced: the first are an old version, which the trash may purge for room as `overwritten`, the link of an entry is Atoll's own move, which the guard removes, and the deletion's promise rests on another copy at the file's path, or on the link of a copy deleted around Atoll.
- An entry a user edited in place by hand still promised the version it was sent with; a restore gave the user its version at its commit, after they had deleted it again; and a purge at the end of the retention read as a loss: the trash's promise is the retention, whatever an entry holds.
- A journal of the instance full left a command waiting for ever, `PurgeSettles` failing: the bound of the model.

### What it leaves out

The guards (`Guards.tla`'s: no decision here waits for one); renames and folders, the folders a restore makes among them, with the mode and owner of the folder of the trash and not its time, by the code of a rename's (`Rename.tla`'s, `Folders.tla`'s, `Nested.tla`'s); the steps of a copy, a move without `RENAME_NOREPLACE`, the power cut and Atoll's own view (`Replication.tla`'s); the SSD cache's own rules, its hand-over of the user part, and the view's move (`Replication.tla`'s, `ViewMove.tla`'s), but their purge at once after its look; the size of each entry and the order of a purge for room (`conformance/vectors/trash_purge.json`); `restore --to`; balance's copy made where its setting wants it, here the other disk's that stays; the chunks of a repair, `renamed` and `unknown`, these two kept as `deleted` is; the guard turned off; a file someone else puts at an entry's name.

## A disk's life: Disks.tla

A model of its own, `Disks.tla` with `MCDisks.tla` (2026-11-26), of §10, §15.13, §18 and §22, the states of §4, and invariants 3, 4, 8, 13 and 16: the third of the maintainer's models by risk (TODO.md). A pool of up to three disks, one file at the same path on some of them, in sync at the start, and, with `Spare`, one more disk the machine has mounted, empty, which the configuration does not list yet. For each disk, what its device does: answering, failing every read past the page cache, unplugged, or dead for good, its data lost; and what its mount path shows: its own filesystem mounted from its device, a mount that outlived that device, the dead superblock a remount gives while the old mount is held (ntfs-3g, §22), an empty mount point, another filesystem, or a clone with its identifier and no marker; whether it is mounted read-only, or holds another pool's marker; whether something holds a file open on its mount; and the versions of the file its own filesystem holds, at the path and in its trash. The catalog: each disk's state in the configuration (in service, `draining`, `failed`, or out of it, `retired`) and from its last check (`online`, `readonly`, `missing`, and missing past `health.missing_grace`), the file's current version and conflict, each copy's row (`ok`, `surplus`, `stale`, `pending`, or none) and version, and the operation planned on each disk. Atoll is one process, as each command takes the pool's lock and each pass holds it (§15.13):

- a pass: the mount check of every disk the configuration lists but a failed one, its verdict recorded (§10); the reprise of what is planned on each disk it can write to (§17.2), undone here, as each job runs in one step with its commit; the walk of each disk it can read, once its confirmation passes, a read of the head past the page cache (§10), and what it found decided (§15.10, §15.12: a copy changed, a modification, the others stale; a copy gone, a deletion; a copy where the catalog has none, recorded; two versions, a conflict); its plan, as `solver.counts`, `isSource`, `planDisk.writable` and `keep` say (§13.4): a draining disk's copies count for nothing and stay sources, one mounted read-only is `readonly` and counts, a missing disk's count within the grace, a copy goes only to a disk in service and online, and the copies past the level are `surplus`, those on a disk missing ranked last; then its jobs, a copy, a refresh or a move to the trash, journaled, then run through the session's handle once the mount it confirmed is still at the path (the device re-checked before each job, §10), the source looked at again and the target's copy compared with its row;
- between passes, the daemon's check of a disk missing for a reason a USB reset gives, or not mounted once its device left, which unmounts it lazily and mounts it again from `/etc/fstab` (§15.13), and time running out a disk's grace (§18);
- the commands, each under the pool's lock (§18, §25): `disk remove --evacuate`, `disk fail`, `disk remove`, with the maintainer's remedies of flaws 29 and 30: every disk's mount checked and recorded, a `surplus` copy counted as an `ok` one, then the disk taken out looked at, but a failed one, refused when it is missing, its look fails or finds what the catalog does not hold, or when it is not `failed` and has an operation planned on it or the file depends on it, as `catalog.ReadDiskHoldsAmong` counts it; of a failed one, what is planned there dropped in the catalog alone, a step of its own before the edit of the configuration; and `disk replace` and `disk add`, refusing a disk whose identifier is a pool disk's or one on the physical disk of a pool disk.

The world acts at any moment: a device unplugged, then plugged again, failing every read, or dead for good; an idle mount whose device left unmounted by systemd; a mount path left empty, given another filesystem or a clone; a disk mounted read-only, or given another pool's marker; a file held open on a mount, by Atoll's own view or a program; the administrator mounting a disk again by hand. A user edits the file directly on a disk, or through mergerfs, whose branch of a draining disk, `NC`, takes a write to a file it holds, or while the disk is away from its path, as on another machine. Atoll's process may be killed between any two steps.

| Invariant | Checks |
|---|---|
| `Inv3_WritesConfirmed` | invariant 3, §10: Atoll writes only to a disk whose own filesystem the session confirmed, through a handle held to that mount, never to an empty mount point, another filesystem or a clone, and only to a disk in the pool, not failed, online |
| `NoCopyOntoLeaving` | §18: a copy goes only to a disk in service: a draining disk is a target for nothing |
| `Inv4_UnknownNeverAbsent` | invariant 4, §10: no copy is decided gone from what a disk missing, unknown or empty showed |
